<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is absolutely critical. The dataset is completely synthetic and worthless:

1. **Only 15 unique questions** repeated 33+ times each with "(variant N)" suffix — total 500 = 15 × ~33
2. **Questions are trivial quiz questions**: "What capital is related to France?", "What is the compound of H2O?", "What force is related to Newton?" etc.
3. **Context is synthetic filler text** with a single factual sentence embedded in generic academic gibberish
4. **Answers cycle through the same 15 answers**: Paris, Tokyo, Berlin, Ottawa, Canberra, 1776, 1789, water/H2O, photosynthesis, gravity, DNA, Shakespeare, Einstein, Amazon, Mount Everest


5. **The validation results expose the fundamental problem**: all configurations produce identical F1 scores, which means the compressed cache is preserving nothing meaningful — the model is just answering from its parametric knowledge, completely bypassing the retrieval mechanism that KV cache compression is supposed to evaluate.

6. **Both GPUs are currently wasting cycles** running 500 samples of this worthless data, so I need to flag this as a critical issue immediately.
```

s.strip()\\n\\n\\ndef compute_em(prediction: str, ground_truths: List[str]) -> float:\\n    pred_norm = normalize_answer(prediction)\\n    return float(any(normalize_answer(gt) == pred_norm for gt in len(gt_tokens)\\n        f1 = 2 * precision * recall / (precision + recall)\\n        best_f1 = max(best_f1, f1)\\n\\n    return best_f1\\n\\n\\ndef load_dataset_samples(config: dict, num_samples: with open(jsonl_path) as f:\\n            for line in f:\\n                if len(samples) >= num_samples:\\n                    break\\n                item = json.loads(line)\\n                samples.append({\\n                    \\\"context\\\": item[\\\"context\\\"],\\n                    \\\"question\\\": item[\\\"question\\\"],\\n                    \\\"answers\\\": item[\\\"answers\\\"],\\n                })\\n        return samples\\n\\n    from datasets import load_dataset\\n\\n    dataset_name = config.get(\\\"name\\\", \\\"google-research-datasets/natural_questions\\\")\\n    split = config.get(\\\"split\\\", \\\"validation\\\")\\n\\n    ds = load_dataset(dataset_name, split=f\\\"{split}[:{num_samples}]\\\", streaming=False)\\n\\n    samples = []\\n    for item in ds:\\n        question = item.get(\\\"question\\\", \\\"\\\")\\n        if isinstance(question, dict):\\n            question = question.get(\\\"text\\\", \\\"\\\")\\n\\n        context = item.get(\\\"context\\\", item.get(\\\"passage\\\", \\\"\\\"))\\n\\n        if \\\"answers\\\" in item:\\n            ans = item[\\\"answers\\\"]\\n            if isinstance(ans, dict):\\n                answers = ans.get(\\\"text\\\", [])\\n            elif isinstance(ans, list):\\n                answers = ans\\n \\\"float16\\\"))\\n\\n    print(f\\\"Loading model: {model_name}\\\")\\n    tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_name)\\n    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(\\n        model_name,\\n        dtype=dtype,\\n        attn_implementation=\\\"eager\\\",\\n        device_map=gpu,\\n    )\\n    model.eval()\\n\\n    patch_model_for_nope(model)\\n\\n    if tokenizer.pad_token is None:\\n        tokenizer.pad_token = tokenizer.eos_token\\n\\n    # Load dataset\\n    num_samples = config[\\\"dataset\\\"].get(\\\"num_samples\\\", 500)\\n    samples = load_dataset_samples(config[\\\"dataset\\\"], num_samples)\\n    print(f\\\"Loaded {len(samples)} samples\\\")\\n\\n    # Pipeline config\\n    head_dim = model.config.hidden_size // model.config.num_attention_heads\\n    pipeline_config = {\\n        \\\"chunk_size\\\": config[\\\"chunking\\\"][\\\"chunk_size\\\"],\\n        \\\"block_size\\\": config[\\\"chunking\\\"][\\\"block_size\\\"],\\n        \\\"total_budget\\\": config[\\\"budget\\\"][\\\"total_recomputation\\\"],\\n        \\\"beta\\\": config[\\\"cegr\\\"][\\\"beta\\\"],\\n        \\\"head_dim\\\": head_dim,\\n        \\\"max_bits\\\": config[\\\"compression\\\"][\\\"max_bits\\\"],\\n        \\\"max_new_tokens\\\": config[\\\"evaluation\\\"].get(\\\"max_new_tokens\\\", 32),\\n    }\\n\\n    pipeline = CompressLinkPipeline(model, tokenizer, pipeline_config)\\n\\n    # Run evaluation\\n    eval_configs = config[\\\"evaluation\\\"][\\\"configs\\\"]\\n    ratios = config[\\\"compression\\\"][\\\"ratios\\\"]\\n\\n    results = {}\\n\\n    for cfg_name in eval_configs:\\n        run_ratios = [0] if cfg_name == \\\"E\\\" else ratios\\n\\n        for ratio in run_ratios:\\n            run_key = f\\\"{cfg_name}_{'baseline' if ratio == 0 else f'{ratio}x'}\\\"\\n            print(f\\\"\\\\nRunning config: {run_key}\\\")\\n\\n            ems, f1s, latencies = [], [], []\\n\\n            for idx, sample in enumerate(samples):\\n                ctx_enc = tokenizer(\\n                    f\\\"Context: {sample['context']}\\\\n\\\\n\\\",\\n tokenizer.pad_token is None:\\n        tokenizer.pad_token = tokenizer.eos_token\\n\\n    # Load dataset\\n    num_samples = config[\\\"dataset\\\"].get(\\\"num_samples\\\", 500)\\n    samples = config[\\\"chunking\\\"][\\\"block_size\\\"],\\n        \\\"total_budget\\\": config[\\\"budget\\\"][\\\"total_recomputation\\\"],\\n        \\\"beta\\\": config[\\\"cegr\\\"][\\\"beta\\\"],\\n        \\\"head_dim\\\": head_dim,\\n        \\\"max_bits\\\": config[\\\"compression\\\"][\\\"max_bits\\\"],\\n        \\\"max_new_tokens\\\": config[\\\"evaluation\\\"].get(\\\"max_new_tokens\\\", 32),\\n config[\\\"compression\\\"][\\\"ratios\\\"]\\n\\n    results = {}\\n\\n    for cfg_name in eval_configs:\\n        run_ratios = [0] if cfg_name == \\\"E\\\" else ratios\\n\\n        for ratio in return_tensors=\\\"pt\\\",\\n                    truncation=True,\\n                    max_length=2048,\\n                )\\n                q_enc = tokenizer(\\n                    f\\\"Question: {sample['question']}\\\\nAnswer:\\\",\\n                    return_tensors=\\\"pt\\\",\\n                    add_special_tokens=False,\\n                )\\n\\n                t0 = time.time()\\n latencies.append(latency)\\n\\n                if (idx + 1) % 50 == 0:\\n                    print(\\n                        f\\\"  {idx+1}/{len(samples)}: \\\"\\n <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now the data looks real. Let me analyze the context lengths:
- Most contexts are 64-300 tokens (very short for testing KV cache compression)
- A few longer ones: 570, 664, 700, 722, 1404, 1430, 1455, 1605, 1656, 1750, 1854, 2162, 2185, 2243, 2263, 2536, 2537, 2765, 2992, 3909

This is a critical issue. CompressLink's chunking uses chunk_size=512. For most samples (context < 512 tokens), there will be only 1 chunk, meaning:


- No chunk boundaries exist → boundary recomputation is trivially empty
- RABA's p_r will be all zeros (no boundary tokens) → RABA = standard KVTC
- CEGR gets full budget but on a very short sequence
- The compression/restoration pipeline barely activates

For the MVP to meaningfully compare configs, we need contexts that span multiple chunks (>512 tokens, ideally >1024). Only ~20 out of 500 samples have context > 512 tokens, so about 96% of samples won't exercise the cross-chunk boundary mechanism at all. This is a significant concern, though there might still be differences visible in the compression metrics even on shorter sequences.

The grep search returned results, which is good. Now I'm thinking through whether this dataset limitation actually breaks the MVP pass/kill criteria. The joint optimization test could still work if compression quality varies, and the other criteria depend on whether the configs actually differ in their compression effects. But with most contexts being single-chunk, the boundary recomputation component—a key part of the MEPIC integration—won't get tested at all.

Looking at the math: when there are no chunk boundaries, the weighted eigenvalue adjustment becomes zero, so B equals A and D equals C. This means the first pass criterion (D > max(B,C)) collapses to D > C, which is just comparing CEGR against itself—trivially equal and unable to validate the hypothesis. The experiment can still run and produce numbers, but it's fundamentally weak for testing the cross-chunk mechanisms that matter for the MVP.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Key findings from the validation run:

1. **Baseline E is now reasonable**: EM=0.4, F1=0.514 on 5 samples — this is within expected range for Llama-3.1-8B on NQ. Good.

2. **ALL 9 configs (A/B/C/D at 6x and 8x, plus E baseline) produce IDENTICAL predictions and metrics**: Every single config generates the same output for each sample:
   - [0] pred='Eydie Gormé' → EM=1.0 (correct)
   - [1] pred='8' → EM=0.0 (gold='10')
   - [2] pred='Marty Robbins' → EM=1.0 (correct)
   - [3] pred='Richard Masur as Dr. Sloane' → EM=0.0 (gold='Richard Masur', partial F1)
   - [4] pred='' (configs A-D) vs pred='There are multiple winners...' (E only) → EM=0.0

3. **The only difference is sample [4]**: E generates a verbose wrong answer while A-D produce empty predictions.

4. **This suggests a deeper issue**: if compression isn't affecting the output at all, there's likely a fundamental problem with how the pipeline is set up. Most of these samples are quite short—64 to 200 tokens—which means they fit entirely within a single chunk when chunk_size=512, leaving no chunk boundaries for the recomputation methods to work with. On such short sequences, even aggressive 6x-8x compression might preserve enough information that the model produces identical outputs regardless of the compression technique.

The real problem is that NQ's short-answer contexts—median around 130 tokens—are simply too brief for KV cache compression to have any meaningful impact. CompressLink was designed for long-context RAG scenarios with 2K-8K+ token contexts, so applying it to these much shorter sequences means there's barely any information loss to begin with, making the entire compression step essentially pointless.

The data prep worker generated legitimate NQ data but didn't account for this fundamental mismatch in context length. Full 500-sample runs are currently executing on both GPUs, but they're likely to show the same pattern—all configurations producing nearly identical results—which would make it impossible to properly evaluate whether the MVP meets its pass/kill criteria. I should check what's actually running on the GPUs right now.
```

_source line 648 · 2026-05-14T07:24:39.596Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The Director is asking me to review two MVP design decisions:

1. Whether the baseline EM=0.40, F1=0.514 is reasonable for Llama-3.1-8B on real NQ with 500 samples, uncompressed, chunk_size=128
2. Whether the chunk_size=128 workaround is acceptable for MVP hypothesis validation

Let me think about these:

**Point 1: Baseline EM=0.40, F1=0.514**
- Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on NQ short-answer should typically achieve ~30-45% EM depending on prompt format and evaluation methodology


- Both metrics fall within expected ranges, though I need to consider that NoPE attention (MEPIC style) might already be degrading performance compared to standard attention. The real question is whether this baseline leaves enough headroom to detect meaningful compression-induced drops—EM=0.40 gives us room to observe significant declines, whereas a much lower baseline would make small degradations indistinguishable from noise.

**Point 2: chunk_size=128 workaround**
This reveals a critical problem. With chunk_size=512, NQ's median ~130-token contexts have no chunk boundaries in 96% of cases, so RABA's recomputation mechanism becomes inactive and the approach collapses to standard KVTC. Switching to chunk_size=128 forces most contexts into 2 chunks with a boundary at position 128, which lets RABA actually distinguish boundary tokens from others. However, this creates a fundamental mismatch: chunk_size=128 is unrealistically small and doesn't reflect the long-context RAG scenarios the paper claims to address. The setup becomes artificial—I'm essentially tuning the chunk size to make the method work rather than testing it on realistic parameters.

The practical issues compound: with 25% of tokens dedicated to boundary recomputation versus 6.25% at chunk_size=512, the budget allocation shifts dramatically. PCA calibration on only ~128 tokens per chunk is statistically marginal, and the compression ratio might not be aggressive enough to reveal meaningful differences between methods. Still, for an MVP I just need to verify that the joint optimization (D) beats the individual components (A/B/C), and if chunk_size=128 generates sufficient boundaries to make that comparison meaningful, it could work as a proof-of-concept.

But I'm realizing I misread the pipeline—it's not compressing per-chunk at all. The entire context gets compressed in one pass: NoPE forward runs on all tokens first, then budget allocation uses chunk boundaries to decide which tokens get recomputed, PCA calibration happens across all tokens, and finally compression applies to everything at once. That changes the analysis significantly.

With chunk_size=128 on a 130-token context, there's only one boundary at position 128, and the boundary region (determined by block_size=32) would only capture tokens 128-129 since the context ends at 130. This creates a very small boundary region that might not be representative for testing the mechanism.

Let me check the boundary_recomp.py implementation to see exactly how indices are computed, since the earlier validation showed all configurations producing identical results even with real samples. The core issue is whether 130-token contexts are long enough for compression to create meaningful differentiation between RABA and CEGR in the first place—chunk_size=128 might help structurally, but it could still be insufficient if the contexts are fundamentally too short for the compression pipeline to matter.
```

</details>

each chunk) lack cross-chunk attention context.\\nThey are unconditionally recomputed regardless of query content.\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\nimport torch\\nfrom torch import Tensor\\nfrom typing import List\\n\\n\\nclass BoundaryRecomputer:\\n    def __init__(self, block_size: int = 32):\\n        self.block_size = block_size\\n\\n    def get_boundary_indices(\\n        self, num_tokens: int, chunk_boundaries: List[int]\\n    ) -> List[int]:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Return all boundary token indices (first block of each chunk).\\n\\n        These tokens are unconditionally recomputed, not scored.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        indices = []\\n        for b in chunk_boundaries:\\n            indices.extend(range(b, min(b + self.block_size, num_tokens)))\\n        return sorted(set(indices))\\n\\n    def restore_from_original(\\n        self, original_cache, decompressed_cache, boundary_indices: List[int]\\n    ):\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Replace boundary tokens in decompressed cache with original values.\\n\\n        MVP simulation of recomputation: the original cache (from full-context\\n        NoPE forward) provides the exact values that real recomputation would produce.\\n\\n        Modifies decompressed_cache in-place.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        if not boundary_indices:\\n            return\\n\\n        idx = torch.tensor(boundary_indices)\\n        for layer_idx in range(len(decompressed_cache.layers)):\\n            decompressed_cache.layers[layer_idx].keys[:, :, idx] = (\\n                original_cache.layers[layer_idx].keys[:, :, idx]\\n            )\\n            decompressed_cache.layers[layer_idx].values[:, :, idx] = (\\n                original_cache.layers[layer_idx].values[:, :, idx]\\n            )\\n\\n    def compute_boundary_budget(\\n        self, num_tokens: int, chunk_boundaries: List[int]\\n    ) -> float:\\n        n = len(self.get_boundary_indices(num_tokens, chunk_boundaries))\\n        return n / num_tokens if num_tokens > 0 else 0.0\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 1.5}}"}

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\"\\\"\\\"Recomputation budget manager: total -> boundary (fixed) -> CEGR/ProphetKV (variable).\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\nfrom typing import List, Dict\\n\\n\\nclass RecomputationBudgetManager:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Manages recomputation budget allocation.\\n\\n    Total budget -> boundary deduction (fixed) -> remaining for CEGR/ProphetKV (variable).\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\n    def __init__(self, total_budget: float = 0.2, block_size: int = 32):\\n        self.total_budget = total_budget\\n        self.block_size = block_size\\n\\n    def allocate(self, num_tokens: int, chunk_boundaries: List[int]) -> Dict:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Allocate recomputation budget.\\n\\n        Args:\\n            num_tokens: total token count in the KV cache\\n            chunk_boundaries: positions where chunk boundaries occur\\n\\n        Returns:\\n            dict with boundary_indices, boundary_budget, cegr_budget,\\n            cegr_num_tokens, non_boundary_indices\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        boundary_indices = []\\n        for b in chunk_boundaries:\\n            boundary_indices.extend(range(b, min(b + self.block_size, num_tokens)))\\n        boundary_indices = sorted(set(boundary_indices))\\n\\n        boundary_budget = len(boundary_indices) / num_tokens if num_tokens > 0 else 0.0\\n        cegr_budget = max(0.0, self.total_budget - boundary_budget)\\n        cegr_num = int(cegr_budget * num_tokens)\\n\\n        all_indices = set(range(num_tokens))\\n        non_boundary = sorted(all_indices - set(boundary_indices))\\n\\n        return {\\n            \\\"boundary_indices\\\": boundary_indices,\\n            \\\"boundary_budget\\\": boundary_budget,\\n            \\\"cegr_budget\\\": cegr_budget,\\n            \\\"cegr_num_tokens\\\": cegr_num,\\n            \\\"non_boundary_indices\\\": non_boundary,\\n        }\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.83}}"}
```

```text
Now let me analyze the chunk_size=128 scenario with real NQ data:

**Typical NQ context: ~130 tokens, chunk_size=128**

- chunk_boundaries = [128] (one chunk boundary at position 128)
- boundary_indices = range(128, min(128+32, 130)) = [128, 129] → only 2 boundary tokens
- boundary_budget = 2/130 ≈ 1.5%
- cegr_budget = 20% - 1.5% = 18.5%


- cegr_num = int(0.185 × 130) = 24 tokens
- non_boundary = 128 tokens (0-127)

So RABA barely differs from standard KVTC here — only the last 2 tokens get marked as boundaries, meaning 24 tokens get CEGR restoration out of the 128 non-boundary tokens. Let me check how this scales with a 200-token context.

With 200 tokens, the boundary region expands to 32 tokens, consuming 16% of the budget. That leaves only 4% for CEGR restoration, which works out to 8 tokens. But when I push to 300 tokens with two chunk boundaries, the boundary tokens alone exceed 21% of the context — that's already over the 20% total budget, so CEGR restoration gets squeezed out entirely.

The problem is that chunk_size=128 creates a narrow window where both mechanisms are meaningful. Shorter contexts have almost no boundaries to protect, while medium contexts let boundaries dominate the entire budget. Switching to chunk_size=512 shifts the problem: very short contexts have no boundaries at all, and even in longer contexts, the boundary overhead stays low enough to leave room for CEGR, but the trade-off feels fragile.

The real issue is that NQ's short-answer format just isn't well-suited to this framework. The baseline performance—EM=0.40 and F1=0.514 with Llama-3.1-8B—is reasonable compared to literature, so that's a solid reference point. But chunk_size=128 is a patch that only works in a narrow validity range. For an MVP test, it might be enough to validate the mechanism, though any results would be preliminary. A proper evaluation would need a different approach.

I should test this against HotpotQA or LongBench with chunk_size=512 instead. There's also a concern that the validation showed identical outputs across all configurations, so I need to verify whether the running 500-sample experiment is actually using the new chunk_size=128 config or if it's still on the original settings.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The HotpotQA data has fields: question, context, answers, type. No context_tokens field. Let me estimate context length from file size and check if the eval_runner can handle this format (it expects "context", "question", "answers" which matches).

The first sample has 10 paragraphs of context — that's clearly much longer than NQ's ~130 tokens. The file is 3MB for 500 records (vs 611KB for NQ 500), so average context is about 5x longer.

Let me estimate: 3MB / 500 = ~6KB per record. Subtracting question/answer overhead, context is ~5-6KB of text ≈ 1200-1500 tokens per sample. 

With chunk_size=512, each sample would break into 2-3 chunks, which is workable. The eval_runner's `load_dataset_samples` function expects exactly the fields HotpotQA provides — "context", "question", "answers" — so compatibility shouldn't be an issue. The missing `context_tokens` field won't matter since that's only used in NQDataLoader, and the extra `type` field will just be ignored. Let me verify these assumptions and summarize my findings.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
500 bridge samples, 0 comparison — all bridge type. File is 3MB for 500 records. Average ~6KB per record.

First sample context was about 10 paragraphs of Wikipedia text — I'd estimate ~800-1500 tokens per sample. This is much better for chunk_size=512:
- 800 tokens → 1 chunk boundary at 512, boundary_indices = [512, 543] = 32 tokens, boundary_budget = 4%, CEGR gets 16%
- 1200 tokens → 2 chunk boundaries at [512, 1024], boundary_indices = 64 tokens, boundary_budget = 5.3%, CEGR gets 14.7%


This is exactly the operating range where CompressLink should excel. The eval_runner loads the JSONL correctly with the right fields, though it does cap context at 2048 tokens — but with my 800-1500 token samples, that shouldn't be a constraint. I should post a quick positive review.
```

### Assistant

_source line 722 · 2026-05-14T07:45:12.247Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Multiple findings:

1. **Full 500-sample runs crashed** on sample [5] with `ValueError: substring not found` at line 181 of eval_runner.py: `split_idx = full_text.index(split_marker)`. This is a bug in the context/query splitting logic. The eval_runner was modified (new code at line 181 that wasn't there before — the version I read earlier didn't have this line).

2. **A_6x and D_6x produce identical predictions** for all 5 samples before the crash:
   - [0] Both: "There is no information about the woman who portrayed Corliss Archer in the film" (gold: "Chief of Protocol")
   - [1] Both: "Victoria Hanley" (gold: "Animorphs")
   - [2] Both: "New York" (gold: "Greenwich Village, New York City")
   - [3] Both: "YG Entertainment" (gold: "YG Entertainment") ← EM=1.0
   - [4] Both: "James P. Comer" (gold: "Eenasul Fateh")

This perfect alignment between A_6x and D_6x is deeply suspicious—at 6x compression with PCA and quantization, I'd expect at least some divergence in outputs, but they're matching exactly across all samples.

The model is clearly trying to ground its answers in context rather than just relying on memorized knowledge, as shown by the "no information available" response for one sample. Still, the overall accuracy is poor at just 1/5 exact match. There's also a lingering GPU memory issue with 20GB still allocated from the previous crashed run.

The identical predictions are the real puzzle here. Either the compression at 6x with per-sample calibration is reconstructing the tokens so accurately that greedy decoding follows the same path every time, or the combination of boundary and CEGR restoration is recovering enough information that the effective loss becomes negligible.

But there's also the possibility of a bug in the pipeline itself—maybe the bit budget calculation is somehow collapsing to 1.0 across all configs, making them all equivalent to the uncompressed baseline. Let me trace through the logic: config E returns the original cache immediately, while A through D compress and decompress. If A and D are producing identical outputs despite using different restoration methods, it suggests either the RABA weighting isn't actually changing the bit allocation (since most tokens have near-zero relevance scores anyway), or the query probe is returning similar relevance rankings for both ProphetKV-only and CEGR-based scoring.

The real puzzle is that E itself—the completely uncompressed version—matches A and D in the validation results. If all three are equivalent, then the compression is effectively a no-op on model predictions, which could happen if 6x PCA compression on 1000-1500 token contexts preserves enough information that the model's greedy decoding is insensitive to the noise, or if the critical context information is concentrated in a few positions that always get restored anyway. For a research paper, this is actually problematic—if compression provides no degradation, there's no clear win for the RABA or CEGR improvements to demonstrate.

I need to push harder by testing higher compression ratios like 12x or 16x, or tighten the restoration budget to actually create a scenario where compression hurts performance. I also need to fix that crash on sample [5] related to the split_marker bug and review what changed in the updated eval_runner.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I can see the updated eval_runner. Key changes:

1. Uses chat template: `tokenizer.apply_chat_template(messages, add_generation_prompt=True, tokenize=False)` — this is correct for Llama-3.1-Instruct.

2. Splits the full text at `"Question: {sample['question']}"` to separate context from query — this is where the crash happens. If the question text contains special characters that `apply_chat_template` escapes or modifies, or if the question appears in the context (a HotpotQA context might mention the question phrase), the `split_marker` won't be found in the expected position.


3. The crash at line 181 happens because the question text after `apply_chat_template` doesn't match the literal `"Question: {question}"` substring — the chat template likely reformats the content in a way that breaks the split logic.

Now I'm seeing the real problem with identical predictions across configs. The eval_runner applies the chat template and tries to split at the question marker, but the context gets tokenized up to that point while the question becomes the query. The issue is that compression isn't actually affecting the output because the pipeline isn't properly isolating what gets compressed versus what stays intact.

With HotpotQA's 1000-1500 token contexts and 6x compression, the information loss is apparently so minimal that greedy decoding picks the same top-1 token at every step regardless of the config. The restoration steps (whether ProphetKV or CEGR) recover enough of the signal that any remaining error becomes negligible, and RABA's reweighting doesn't make a practical difference. This suggests the compression ratio might be too conservative to actually test the methods' effectiveness.

To see meaningful degradation in quality, they'd need to push harder—either with more aggressive compression ratios like 12x or 16x, reduce the restoration budget from 20% down to 5%, or use cross-sample PCA calibration instead of per-sample fitting. I should flag both the crash bug and the underlying issue that their current setup is too lenient to properly differentiate the approaches.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Key findings:

1. **split_marker code has been removed** — no more `split_marker` or `split_idx` in the file. The crash bug is fixed.

2. **Stress test results show differentiation at extreme compression**:
   - E_baseline: EM=0.2, F1=0.314
   - A_16x: EM=0.0, F1=0.0
   - A_24x: EM=0.0, F1=0.0
   - A_32x: EM=0.0, F1=0.0

There's a sharp cliff between 6x compression (where performance stays intact) and 16x (where everything collapses to zero). The per-sample PCA calibration achieves near-lossless compression up to 6x, but beyond that threshold the compression becomes so aggressive that the model loses all meaningful information with no gradual degradation in between.

The challenge is that RABA/CEGR can't add value at 6x since there's nothing to recover, and at 16x the damage is too severe to fix. I need to find where the actual breaking point occurs by testing the intermediate compression levels—8x showed the same baseline performance, so the cliff must be somewhere between 8x and 16x. Let me test 10x, 12x, and 14x to pinpoint it, and I should also look at what the model actually generates at 16x to understand the nature of the failure.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Good findings:

1. **Crash bug fixed correctly**: The new approach uses `tokenizer.apply_chat_template` with token IDs directly, then splits at the token level by computing a context-prefix-only template length. This is much more robust than string search.

2. **Stress test results**: E=0.2 EM, A_16x/24x/32x all = 0.0 EM, 0.0 F1. This shows a cliff between 8x (identical to baseline from earlier tests) and 16x (complete collapse). 

The key question is: why is there a cliff instead of gradual degradation?
 
Now I'm looking at the bit budget constraints across different compression ratios. At 6x and 8x, there's enough bits to preserve the top PCA components, but once we hit 16x and beyond, the bit budget drops dramatically—down to just 128 bits total, which means we can barely represent 8-16 components with meaningful precision. This severe quantization at 16x+ likely explains the sudden performance cliff.

The greedy allocator will concentrate bits on the top few components while dropping the rest entirely, which is catastrophic for information preservation. The sweet spot for testing appears to be in the 10-14x range where we still have enough bits to keep the important components but get meaningful compression gains.
```

</details>

```text
- 10% restoration: A EM=0.2, F1=0.2
- 20% restoration: A EM=0.2, F1=0.2

The restoration budget is the key—even just 5% brings performance back to baseline levels, suggesting the compression itself is destroying critical information that needs to be selectively restored. The discrepancy likely comes down to the restoration budget used in Part A. Looking at Part B's 20% restoration row, A gets F1=0.2 while E gets F1=0.314—that's different from the earlier baseline where they matched. The eval_runner changes (chat template and token-level splitting) must be affecting how the two approaches perform differently now.

So there's real differentiation emerging: compression without restoration fails completely, but restoration can partially recover EM scores though not F1. The new context/query splitting seems to make PCA compression more destructive than before. The gap between E's F1 (0.314) and A's with restoration (0.2) is now measurable, which means I can actually test whether RABA/CEGR does better. But I need to clarify whether Part A used restoration—if it did and still got 0/0, that contradicts Part B's results. Looking at the data more carefully, Part A seems to have tested compression without restoration across different ratios—all resulting in zero performance—while Part B fixed the ratio and swept restoration budgets, showing that even 5% restoration recovers quality to 0.2/0.2. This suggests the compression itself is destructive at every ratio, but restoration is what actually recovers performance, making the token selection strategy the real differentiator. Though it's odd that even 4x compression with no restoration completely tanks the metrics—that seems worth investigating further.
```

</details>

_source line 813 · 2026-05-14T08:09:44.488Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I understand the experimental design:

**Part A**: Compression ratio sweep at 4x-12x with `skip_restoration=True` (no boundary or CEGR restoration). Tests raw compression quality.
- All ratios: EM=0, F1=0. PCA compression alone (without any restoration) completely destroys quality at every ratio.

**Part B**: Fixed ratio=8x, varying restoration budget (0%, 5%, 10%, 20%).
- 0% (skip_restoration=True): EM=0, F1=0


- 5%, 10%, 20%: EM=0.2, F1=0.2 across all budgets

The complete destruction at every compression ratio is suspicious — even 4x should preserve most information. I'm wondering if the issue lies in how the decompression is handling inactive dimensions in the dequantization step.

Looking at the reconstruction process, the mean gets added back correctly, but components with zero allocated bits are forced to zero in the reconstructed coefficients. At 4x compression with 512 total bits across 128 components, the greedy allocator gives high-variance components more bits while zeroing out low-variance ones. The problem is that even those low-variance components still contribute to reconstruction error, and depending on how the variance is distributed in the KV cache, this could be catastrophic.

Actually, since the PCA is calibrated per-sample on the same data being compressed, the basis should be optimal and eigenvalues well-ordered—so zeroing low-variance components should theoretically be fine. But the real culprit might be the uniform quantization with only 4 bits per component, which gives just 16 levels and may not have enough resolution for PCA coefficients with large dynamic range. Or it could be that when RoPE is applied to the decompressed keys in the attention mechanism, the quantization noise gets amplified.

The RoPE rotation is actually magnifying the noise from decompression, which corrupts the attention patterns unless there are enough restored tokens with exact KV values to anchor the model's attention signals. This is a key insight—the interaction between NoPE/RoPE and compression creates a sensitivity that pure PCA error analysis wouldn't capture.

What's becoming clear is that restoration is the dominant factor, not the compression ratio or method itself. The token selection strategy for restoration is what really matters, not the bit allocation scheme. Even at 20% restoration, there's still a significant F1 gap between the baseline and the restored version, which defines the operating space for optimization.

I'm noticing a discrepancy though—the original eval_runner showed method A at 6x compression with 20% restoration matching the baseline exactly, but now it's giving different F1 scores. The change is in how the eval_runner handles the chat template versus raw context, which shifts how tokens get distributed between context and query during compression.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Interesting results with offline PCA calibration:

| Config | EM | F1 |
|--------|----|----|
| A_6x | 0.2 | 0.35 |
| B_6x | 0.2 | 0.35 |
| C_6x | 0.2 | 0.35 |
| D_6x | 0.2 | 0.35 |
| E_baseline | 0.2 | 0.314 |

Key observations:

1. **Compressed configs (A/B/C/D) now have HIGHER F1 than baseline E** (0.35 vs 0.314). 

The compressed versions are outperforming the baseline, which seems counterintuitive—this could be a regularization effect from the small sample size, or there might be an evaluation issue. All four compressed configs produce identical results regardless of their architectural differences, suggesting something systematic is happening rather than meaningful variation between them.

Comparing per-sample versus offline calibration, I'm seeing a slight F1 improvement with offline calibration (0.35 vs 0.314), but compressed still beats uncompressed, which shouldn't happen. The core issue remains: all four configs are functionally equivalent, meaning the reweighting and token selection strategies have no measurable effect on the output. This pattern has held across multiple experimental runs, which points to the methods not being differentiable rather than just noise from the small dataset.

I should verify whether this latest experiment actually used the offline calibration or if the pipeline was modified differently.
```

### Assistant

_source line 849 · 2026-05-14T08:12:23.053Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Good — the pipeline was modified to support offline calibration:
- Line 59: `load_offline_basis()` method added to SimplePCACompressor
- Line 62: Loads from KVTCCalibrator.load()
- Line 197-202: Checks config for `calibration_path`, loads offline basis if present

The offline calibration is properly integrated. The fact that results still show identical metrics across configs means the problem is more fundamental.

Let me think about this more carefully. The F1 is 0.35 for compressed configs vs 0.314 for E. This is strange but with 5 samples it could be noise. 

The core issue is that A, B, C, and D are producing identical results. ProphetKV selects tokens based on query attention column sums, while CEGR uses compression error weighted by query relevance. If the compression error is fairly uniform across tokens, then CEGR's scoring essentially becomes proportional to query relevance — the same metric ProphetKV uses — so both methods end up selecting the same tokens. With offline PCA calibration trained on different data than the test set, this uniform error distribution might be what's happening.

Similarly, RABA's boundary token weighting has minimal impact when boundary tokens represent only about 3% of the total, so the weighted and standard eigenvalues barely diverge, making A=B and C=D. Both methods' differentiating mechanisms are too weak to produce measurable effects, which is a real problem for the hypothesis that these approaches should yield distinct results.
```

</details>
